It's like my brain thinks I still have a whole crew waiting on me to get things done, and I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Like, when I help out with these volunteer storytelling flyers, or when I'm just writing down my garden notes... I'll end up missing a date, or I'll leave out somebody's name. Or I'll measure something out wrong in the yard, even though I really thought I looked it over and had it right. I mean, I like to chat with folks, sure, but I wouldn't say I talk too much or take over the whole conversation. I'm usually pretty good about just letting other people have their say.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with sitting still, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some issues.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, what brings me in is... I guess since I retired and stopped working, I just can't seem to slow down. I'm up and moving from one little job around the house to the next, all day long. It's like my brain thinks I still have a whole crew waiting on me to get things done, and I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I like to keep busy, but I wouldn't say I just do things without thinking. I usually have a plan for what I'm working on around the house, I just... have a hard time stopping once I start. But I'm not really making rash decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. Like, when I help out with these volunteer storytelling flyers, or when I'm just writing down my garden notes... I'll end up missing a date, or I'll leave out somebody's name. Or I'll measure something out wrong in the yard, even though I really thought I looked it over and had it right. Just little careless mistakes like that, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, if I have to sit through something quiet, like a meeting or a talk at church, I just can't seem to stay still. I end up shifting around in my seat, tapping my foot, or feeling like I just need to get up and stretch. Even when I know I'm supposed to just sit there and listen, it's really hard for me to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing my pencils, or just... calling out answers in class without waiting. They constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been like this for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting overly angry or frustrated when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might get a little annoyed if the weather ruins my plans for the garden, but... I don't really lose my temper or get overly angry. I think I handle my emotions pretty well, all things considered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to chat with folks, sure, but I wouldn't say I talk too much or take over the whole conversation. I'm usually pretty good about just letting other people have their say.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with sitting still, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some issues. My wife has actually had to take over keeping the calendar, and she ends up checking behind me a lot just to make sure things are handled. And outside the house... well, I've had to apologize to the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. Just missing things or not finishing what I started. So, yes, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your adult life, not just since you retired?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been my whole life, really, not just since I retired. I mean, even way back in grade school, my teachers would tell my parents I was a bright kid but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers in class... and they constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's always been this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with substance use or addiction?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've never had any issues with drugs or alcohol or anything like that. It's just never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I have much trouble with that part of things. I usually know what needs to be done and can get my tools or whatever lined up just fine. Like I said before, it's more the finishing part that gets me. But just organizing it all to begin with? No, I haven't really noticed a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or in your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does cause some issues. At home, my wife has basically had to take over keeping the calendar for us, and she's always having to check behind me to make sure I actually followed through on things. And socially... well, I've had to apologize to the folks at the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. So, yeah, it definitely gets in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's like my brain thinks I still have a whole crew waiting on me to get things done, and I just can't sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I like to keep busy, but I wouldn't say I just do things without thinking. I usually have a plan for what I'm working on around the house, I just... have a hard time stopping once I start. But I'm not really making rash decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. Like, when I help out with these volunteer storytelling flyers, or when I'm just writing down my garden notes... I'll end up missing a date, or I'll leave out somebody's name. Or I'll measure something out wrong in the yard, even though I really thought I looked it over and had it right. Just little careless mistakes like that, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, if I have to sit through something quiet, like a meeting or a talk at church, I just can't seem to stay still. I end up shifting around in my seat, tapping my foot, or feeling like I just need to get up and stretch. Even when I know I'm supposed to just sit there and listen, it's really hard for me to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing my pencils, or just... calling out answers in class without waiting. They constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been like this for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting overly angry or frustrated when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might get a little annoyed if the weather ruins my plans for the garden, but... I don't really lose my temper or get overly angry. I think I handle my emotions pretty well, all things considered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to chat with folks, sure, but I wouldn't say I talk too much or take over the whole conversation. I'm usually pretty good about just letting other people have their say.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with sitting still, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some issues. My wife has actually had to take over keeping the calendar, and she ends up checking behind me a lot just to make sure things are handled. And outside the house... well, I've had to apologize to the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. Just missing things or not finishing what I started. So, yes, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your adult life, not just since you retired?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been my whole life, really, not just since I retired. I mean, even way back in grade school, my teachers would tell my parents I was a bright kid but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers in class... and they constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's always been this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with substance use or addiction?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've never had any issues with drugs or alcohol or anything like that. It's just never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I have much trouble with that part of things. I usually know what needs to be done and can get my tools or whatever lined up just fine. Like I said before, it's more the finishing part that gets me. But just organizing it all to begin with? No, I haven't really noticed a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or in your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does cause some issues. At home, my wife has basically had to take over keeping the calendar for us, and she's always having to check behind me to make sure I actually followed through on things. And socially... well, I've had to apologize to the folks at the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. So, yeah, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any close relationships end because of your difficulties with following through on commitments or your restless behavior?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, like I said, my wife gets frustrated with me sometimes, but we're still going strong. And my friends... well, they might get annoyed if I drop the ball on something, but nobody has ever actually cut ties with me over it. So, no, I haven't lost any close relationships because of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention in class when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. I mean, I like to keep busy, but I wouldn't say I just do things without thinking. I usually have a plan for what I'm working on around the house, I just... have a hard time stopping once I start. But I'm not really making rash decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. Like, when I help out with these volunteer storytelling flyers, or when I'm just writing down my garden notes... I'll end up missing a date, or I'll leave out somebody's name. Or I'll measure something out wrong in the yard, even though I really thought I looked it over and had it right. Just little careless mistakes like that, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, if I have to sit through something quiet, like a meeting or a talk at church, I just can't seem to stay still. I end up shifting around in my seat, tapping my foot, or feeling like I just need to get up and stretch. Even when I know I'm supposed to just sit there and listen, it's really hard for me to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing my pencils, or just... calling out answers in class without waiting. They constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been like this for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting overly angry or frustrated when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might get a little annoyed if the weather ruins my plans for the garden, but... I don't really lose my temper or get overly angry. I think I handle my emotions pretty well, all things considered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to chat with folks, sure, but I wouldn't say I talk too much or take over the whole conversation. I'm usually pretty good about just letting other people have their say.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with sitting still, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some issues. My wife has actually had to take over keeping the calendar, and she ends up checking behind me a lot just to make sure things are handled. And outside the house... well, I've had to apologize to the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. Just missing things or not finishing what I started. So, yes, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your adult life, not just since you retired?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been my whole life, really, not just since I retired. I mean, even way back in grade school, my teachers would tell my parents I was a bright kid but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers in class... and they constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's always been this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with substance use or addiction?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've never had any issues with drugs or alcohol or anything like that. It's just never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I have much trouble with that part of things. I usually know what needs to be done and can get my tools or whatever lined up just fine. Like I said before, it's more the finishing part that gets me. But just organizing it all to begin with? No, I haven't really noticed a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or in your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does cause some issues. At home, my wife has basically had to take over keeping the calendar for us, and she's always having to check behind me to make sure I actually followed through on things. And socially... well, I've had to apologize to the folks at the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. So, yeah, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any close relationships end because of your difficulties with following through on commitments or your restless behavior?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, like I said, my wife gets frustrated with me sometimes, but we're still going strong. And my friends... well, they might get annoyed if I drop the ball on something, but nobody has ever actually cut ties with me over it. So, no, I haven't lost any close relationships because of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention in class when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Even way back in grade school, I remember my teachers saying I was a bright kid, but... well, just scattered. I couldn't really keep my focus. I was always losing my pencils, or I'd just call out the answers in the middle of class. And they were always having to remind me to actually finish my work before they'd let me go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to hold a job or maintain steady employment due to your difficulties with restlessness, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: No, I always managed to keep steady work. I'm retired now, but... well, during my working years, I never got fired over it or anything like that. I mean, I might have frustrated a boss or two if I rushed through a task and made a mistake, but it never actually cost me a job. I always held onto my employment just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have held you back from achieving your full potential in life or pursuing things you truly wanted to do?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really think so. I mean, I'm retired now, and looking back... I feel like I've had a pretty good life. I did the work I needed to do, raised a family, and I'm happy with where I ended up. Sure, things were a little messy sometimes, but I don't feel like I missed out on anything big or didn't reach my potential. I just kind of lived my life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever interfered with your ability to engage in leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose they do affect my downtime a bit. I don't really do well with quiet hobbies for very long anymore. Like, even if I'm just sitting down to read a book or do a little sketching, I can't just stay still and quiet. I'll start humming to myself, or I'll catch myself talking out loud. And usually, before too long, I just end up getting up to go mess with something else around the house. So, yeah, sitting quietly with a hobby is pretty tough for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I like to keep busy, but I wouldn't say I just do things without thinking. I usually have a plan for what I'm working on around the house, I just... have a hard time stopping once I start. But I'm not really making rash decisions or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. Like, when I help out with these volunteer storytelling flyers, or when I'm just writing down my garden notes... I'll end up missing a date, or I'll leave out somebody's name. Or I'll measure something out wrong in the yard, even though I really thought I looked it over and had it right. Just little careless mistakes like that, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, if I have to sit through something quiet, like a meeting or a talk at church, I just can't seem to stay still. I end up shifting around in my seat, tapping my foot, or feeling like I just need to get up and stretch. Even when I know I'm supposed to just sit there and listen, it's really hard for me to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in grade school, my teachers used to say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing my pencils, or just... calling out answers in class without waiting. They constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been like this for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting overly angry or frustrated when things don't go as planned?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might get a little annoyed if the weather ruins my plans for the garden, but... I don't really lose my temper or get overly angry. I think I handle my emotions pretty well, all things considered.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to chat with folks, sure, but I wouldn't say I talk too much or take over the whole conversation. I'm usually pretty good about just letting other people have their say.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with sitting still, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some issues. My wife has actually had to take over keeping the calendar, and she ends up checking behind me a lot just to make sure things are handled. And outside the house... well, I've had to apologize to the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. Just missing things or not finishing what I started. So, yes, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your adult life, not just since you retired?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been my whole life, really, not just since I retired. I mean, even way back in grade school, my teachers would tell my parents I was a bright kid but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers in class... and they constantly had to give me reminders to actually finish my work before I could go out for recess. So, yeah, it's always been this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with substance use or addiction?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've never had any issues with drugs or alcohol or anything like that. It's just never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I have much trouble with that part of things. I usually know what needs to be done and can get my tools or whatever lined up just fine. Like I said before, it's more the finishing part that gets me. But just organizing it all to begin with? No, I haven't really noticed a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or in your social relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does cause some issues. At home, my wife has basically had to take over keeping the calendar for us, and she's always having to check behind me to make sure I actually followed through on things. And socially... well, I've had to apologize to the folks at the community center and my garden group more than once for letting people down. So, yeah, it definitely gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any close relationships end because of your difficulties with following through on commitments or your restless behavior?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, like I said, my wife gets frustrated with me sometimes, but we're still going strong. And my friends... well, they might get annoyed if I drop the ball on something, but nobody has ever actually cut ties with me over it. So, no, I haven't lost any close relationships because of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention in class when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely did. Even way back in grade school, I remember my teachers saying I was a bright kid, but... well, just scattered. I couldn't really keep my focus. I was always losing my pencils, or I'd just call out the answers in the middle of class. And they were always having to remind me to actually finish my work before they'd let me go out for recess. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to hold a job or maintain steady employment due to your difficulties with restlessness, completing tasks, or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: No, I always managed to keep steady work. I'm retired now, but... well, during my working years, I never got fired over it or anything like that. I mean, I might have frustrated a boss or two if I rushed through a task and made a mistake, but it never actually cost me a job. I always held onto my employment just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have held you back from achieving your full potential in life or pursuing things you truly wanted to do?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really think so. I mean, I'm retired now, and looking back... I feel like I've had a pretty good life. I did the work I needed to do, raised a family, and I'm happy with where I ended up. Sure, things were a little messy sometimes, but I don't feel like I missed out on anything big or didn't reach my potential. I just kind of lived my life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever interfered with your ability to engage in leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose they do affect my downtime a bit. I don't really do well with quiet hobbies for very long anymore. Like, even if I'm just sitting down to read a book or do a little sketching, I can't just stay still and quiet. I'll start humming to myself, or I'll catch myself talking out loud. And usually, before too long, I just end up getting up to go mess with something else around the house. So, yeah, sitting quietly with a hobby is pretty tough for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in at least two settings, such as at home and in social situations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say so, no. I mean, I notice the fidgeting and having a hard time sitting still at home, like I mentioned with my hobbies, but... it's not really a big issue when I'm out with other people or doing things socially. I might be a little restless, but it's not causing any real problems in different parts of my life. It's mostly just how I am around the house.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
